=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a permutation P=(P_1,P_2,\dots,P_N) of (1,2,\dots,N).
Consider the following operations k\ (k=2,3,\dots,N) on this permutation.

- Operation k: For i=1,2,\dots,k-1 in this order, if P_i > P_{i+1}, swap the values of the i-th and (i+1)-th elements of P.

You are also given a non-decreasing sequence A=(A_1,A_2,\dots,A_M)\ (2 \leq A_i \leq N) of length M.
For each i=1,2,\dots,M, find the inversion number of P after applying the operations A_1, A_2, \dots, A_i in this order.

 What is the inversion number of a sequence?

The inversion number of a sequence x=(x_1,x_2,\dots,x_n) of length n is the number of pairs of integers (i,j)\ (1\leq i < j \leq n) such that x_i > x_j.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
P_1 P_2 \dots P_N
M
A_1 A_2 \dots A_M

Output

Print M lines. The k-th line should contain the answer to the problem for i=k.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq M \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 2 \leq A_i \leq N
- P is a permutation of (1,2,\dots,N).
- A_i \leq A_{i+1} for i=1,2,\dots,M-1.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
3 2 4 1 6 5
2
4 6

Sample Output 1

3
1

First, operation 4 is performed. During this, P changes as follows: (3,2,4,1,6,5) \rightarrow (2,3,4,1,6,5) \rightarrow (2,3,4,1,6,5) \rightarrow (2,3,1,4,6,5). The inversion number of P afterward is 3.
Next, operation 6 is performed, where P eventually becomes (2,1,3,4,5,6), whose inversion number is 1.

Sample Input 2

20
12 14 16 8 7 15 19 6 18 5 13 9 10 17 4 1 11 20 2 3
15
3 4 6 8 8 9 10 12 13 15 18 18 19 19 20

Sample Output 2

117
116
113
110
108
105
103
99
94
87
79
72
65
58
51

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

- Maintain current maximum m among processed elements, at the right end of the processed sequence.

Thus after processing all, the maximum of all is at position k. The elements before it are the result of applying this process to the sequence excluding? Thus operation k on current array:
Let p = position of the maximum value among first k elements (i.e., value? Since P is permutation, maximum value in prefix is some value v; p = its current position). Then operation k:
- Apply operation p-1 to the prefix of length p-1 (if p>1) (because the first p-1 comparisons are T_{p-1}).
- Move the maximum element from position p to position k, shifting elements p+1..k one position left (i.e., remove it and append to end of prefix).
Wait if p=k, then no shifting after p; operation k = T_{k-1} on first k-1, then compare pos k-1,k: max at k? If p=k, max already at end, compare pos k-1 with max no swap. So operation k = T_{k-1} on first k-1, max remains at k. This fits: remove max from p=k and append to end (no change), shift none.

So operation k is: find current position p of the maximum element in prefix [1..k]. This recursive structure may help. Since values are permutation, maximum among first k is the largest value whose current position <= k. Let's denote value v = max{P_j | j<=k}. Actually operation k on current array:
- Let v be maximum value in first k.
- Move v to position k.
- Then apply operation (pos(v)-1) to the prefix before v's original position.
But after moving v to k, positions p+1..k shift left; the prefix before p unchanged. Then operation p-1 applies to positions 1..p-1. This is independent of the shift. So operation k can be decomposed into:
1. Find current position p of max value in prefix k.
2. Move that value to position k (remove at p, insert at k; elements p+1..k shift left).
3. Recursively perform operation p-1 on the first p-1 elements (which are unchanged by step 2).

This is reminiscent of processing values in decreasing order. Since the maximum value in prefix k is likely some value v. Operation k moves v to the right end of prefix k. Then recursion on prefix before v. This is exactly like building a tree: For a given operation k, the element moved to position k is the maximum among first k. Then the operation on prefix before its position will move another maximum, etc. The sequence of swaps in operation k consists of:
- The maximum v moves from p to k, causing k-p swaps (one with each element to its right in prefix). Wait if p<k, v swaps with x_{p+1},...,x_k: k-p swaps. These swaps are all performed after reaching p, and each is a swap. Then recursively operation p-1 performs some swaps within positions 1..p-1. Great. So number of swaps in operation k equals (k - position_of_max_in_prefix_k) + swaps from operation (position_of_max - 1) on the prefix before that max. This is recursive. Maybe there is a connection to Cartesian tree. The LTR maxima form a chain in the max-Cartesian tree. Operation T(k) rotates along the right spine? Let's explore.

T(empty)=empty.

For a sequence S, F(S) = F(L) + R + root. This is exactly the result of one "cycle" on the Cartesian tree: root moves to the end, right subtree remains before root, left subtree is recursively transformed. If we apply F repeatedly to the same sequence S, what happens? This is standard bubble sort pass repeated; after enough passes, sequence sorted. Maybe the entire sequence of operations with nondecreasing k can be represented by applying F to prefixes. Since A nondecreasing, we apply F to prefixes of lengths A_i. - Find max m in prefix k.
- Move m to position k (delete at p, insert at k), shifting R left.
- Recursively apply F to L (prefix before m).
This is exactly the same. The number of swaps is |R| + swaps(F(L)). Note |R| = k-p.

Now, if we apply F to prefix k many times, after first application, the max m is at position k. Then F again on prefix k: max of prefix k is still m (since m is max of whole prefix and at end), L = prefix k-1, R empty, so F(S) = F(prefix k-1) + [m]. This suggests a recursive algorithm for processing counts c_k:
Define a function solve(k, c): apply F to prefix k c times. If c=0 return. If c>0:
- Let S = current prefix k. Let m = max(S), position p. Split L,R.
- First application: swaps += |R|; update S = F(L) + R + [m] = (apply F to L once) + R + [m].
- Remaining c-1 applications: F on S with max m at end: S = F(prefix k-1) + [m]. This is equivalent to applying F to prefix k-1 c-1 times, with m fixed at end.
So total effect of c applications to prefix k:
1. Apply F once to L (recursively) and move m to end (swaps |R|).
2. Apply F to prefix k-1 c-1 times.
But note after step 1, the prefix k-1 is F(L)+R. Step 2 applies F to this entire prefix k-1 c-1 times. This is not just applying to L; it includes R. So we need process prefix k-1 with c-1 operations after the first F(k). This is like a recursion on k decreasing with count c-1, plus a recursive F(L) from the first operation.

Maybe we can combine counts. Let's define a recursive function that processes a run of c operations on a prefix length k, and returns the resulting sequence of that prefix and swaps. It may call itself on smaller prefixes. Since k decreases by at least 1 for the c-1 part, and p-1 < k for the recursive F(L), the recursion depth could be O(N) per original run, but maybe we can memoize? Alternative: Use the sequence transformation F(S)=F(L)+R+max. This is exactly "move max to end and recursively process left". If we maintain the sequence as a balanced binary tree with implicit treap, applying F to a prefix can be done by:
- Split prefix k into L, [m], R where m is maximum in prefix.
- Recursively apply F to L.
- Concatenate F(L), R, [m].
But recursive application to L may require finding max in L and recursing again. In the worst case, applying F to a reverse sorted prefix of length N would recurse N times (each max at first, R large) and move many elements. But if we implement F recursively, it will perform O(number of left-to-right maxima) operations, which can be O(N) for one F. Repeating F(N) N times would be O(N^2). Need avoid.

But maybe we can apply F to a prefix in O(log N) using a lazy representation of the Cartesian tree. Let's analyze F in terms of Cartesian tree.

For a sequence S, the max-Cartesian tree is a binary tree where root = max, left child = Cartesian tree of L, right child = Cartesian tree of R. Inorder traversal gives S. What is F(S) = F(L) + R + max in terms of Cartesian tree?
- F(L) is the transformation of the left subtree's sequence.
- R is the right subtree's sequence (unchanged).
- max is root.
So F(S) sequence corresponds to a tree where? Inorder of a tree T' = F(L) + R + root. This is not a standard Cartesian tree of the new sequence? But if we take the Cartesian tree of the new sequence, what is it? Since root max is at the end, in the new sequence max is last, so in its Cartesian tree, root is still max and has no right child? Wait root of Cartesian tree is the maximum of the sequence. In F(S), max is at the end, so root is the last element; its left child is the Cartesian tree of the prefix F(L)+R, right child empty. Thus the Cartesian tree of F(S) is obtained by taking the Cartesian tree of F(L)+R and making it the left child of root. But F(L)+R is not simply the old tree with left subtree transformed? Let's see.

Maybe F corresponds to a rotation on the Cartesian tree's right spine. Let's examine max-Cartesian tree of S. Root m, left subtree L_tree, right subtree R_tree. Inorder: L_tree, m, R_tree. F(S) inorder: F(L), R, m. The new root is m, with left subtree representing F(L)+R, right empty. The left subtree's Cartesian tree has root = max of F(L)+R. What is max of F(L)+R? It is max(max(L), max(R)) = second maximum of S. In the original tree, the second maximum is either in L or R. Specifically, if R nonempty, max(R) is the root of R_tree; if R empty, max(L) is root of L_tree. So the new left subtree root is the maximum of the old right subtree if R nonempty, else the old left subtree root. - Each record m_s moves right by |B_s| positions? m1 pos1 -> pos2 (right1), m2 pos3 -> pos4 (right1), m3 pos5 -> pos6 (right1). In general record m_s moves right by |B_s|? Since B_s moves before it, m_s shifts right by |B_s|. But if B_s empty, no move. So records move right by length of following block.

Thus F on prefix k is equivalent to:
- For each position i in prefix, if P_i is a LTR maximum, it moves right by the length of the contiguous block of non-records immediately after it (until next record); if P_i is not a LTR maximum, it moves left by 1.
This is exactly one pass of bubble sort: smaller elements move left one, larger elements move right over consecutive smaller elements.

Now, how to maintain this under many operations? Maybe we can track each element's position and the number of times it has moved left. Since each operation on a prefix moves each non-record in that prefix left by 1, and records right. But record status changes after each operation. However, maybe there is an invariant involving values: An element moves left in an operation iff it is not the maximum among the prefix up to its position? Actually LTR maximum in prefix means value greater than all elements before it in the current prefix. Since values are unique, an element is a LTR maximum in the prefix iff it is the maximum among elements from start to its position. This is equivalent to its value being greater than all elements to its left within the prefix. After operation, elements move.

Could we compute inversion count after each operation using only initial positions and operation lengths? Let's explore pair crossing.

In one bubble pass on prefix k, each inversion pair (a>b) with both in prefix and a initially left of b may or may not swap. Actually in one pass, a larger element a moves right across all consecutive smaller elements until it meets a larger element. So it swaps with all elements to its right that are smaller and before the next larger element. This is exactly the block after a record. Over repeated passes, a larger element can continue moving right across smaller elements that were in later blocks? In standard bubble sort, a larger element moves right across all smaller elements over multiple passes? Example [3,1,5,4,6,2], value 5 (record) in first pass moves right across block [4]? Wait records: 3,5,6. m2=5, B2=[4], so 5 moves right by 1 to pos4? Initial [3,1,5,4,6,2] positions: 3 pos1,1 pos2,5 pos3,4 pos4,6 pos5,2 pos6. F1 -> [1,3,4,5,2,6]: 5 pos4 (right1). In F2, records: 1,3,4,5,6. 5 is record with following block [2]? Actually sequence [1,3,4,5,2,6], records 1,3,4,5,6; block after 5 before6 is [2], so 5 moves right by 1 to pos5 in F2. So 5 moves right across 4 then 2 over two passes. Thus a larger element moves right across smaller elements one block per pass, not all at once if separated by even larger elements? Actually 5 initially had 6 to its right; 6 is larger, so 5 cannot pass 6. It passes 4 in first pass, then 2 in second, ending before 6. So each element moves right across smaller elements until blocked by larger element; over passes, it crosses a number of smaller elements equal to inversions with larger? This is bubble sort.

Maybe we can model each value's position over time using its rank. For a fixed value v, it moves left by 1 in a pass iff it is not a LTR maximum in the prefix at that time. It moves right by some amount if it is a LTR maximum. But perhaps the total number of swaps (inversion decrease) can be computed by considering "inversion table" and operation k as decrementing something.

Let's look at inversion count after operation k. Since swaps = k - (#LTR maxima in prefix k). The number of LTR maxima in a sequence of length k equals the number of "records" (left-to-right maxima). There is a known data structure to maintain number of records under a transformation? Maybe we can maintain the sequence in a balanced BST where each node stores whether it is a record (greater than max of prefix). Operation F on prefix k changes records: after F, the records of the prefix are exactly the records of the blocks plus the old records? Let's derive a recurrence for the number of records after F.

Let S = m1 B1 m2 B2 ... mt Bt. Let f(S) = number of LTR maxima in S = t (by definition records). Let g(S) = number of LTR maxima in F(S). Can we express g in terms of blocks?
F(S) = B1 m1 B2 m2 ... Bt mt.
Records of F(S):
- Within B1: records of B1 (call r(B1)).
- Then m1: since m1 > all elements in B1? Yes B1 elements < m1, so m1 is a record.
- Then B2: elements in B2 are < m2 but may be > m1. The current maximum before B2 is m1. So records within B2 that are greater than m1 (i.e., records of B2 after threshold m1) become records. Then m2 is > all previous? m2 > m1 and > B2, so m2 is a record.
- Then B3: current max m2, records in B3 greater than m2? But B3 elements < m3, can be > m2? Example B3=[2] < m2=5? no. Could B3 contain element > m2? If x in B3 after m2 before m3, if x > m2, then x would be a record and m3 would be > x, so x would be a LTR maximum, contradiction because m3 is next record? Wait m3 is next record after m2, so no element between m2 and m3 can be > m2. Thus all elements in B_s for s>=2 are < m_{s-1}? Let's check: B_s is after m_s before m_{s+1}. Since m_{s+1} is the next record after m_s, all elements between m_s and m_{s+1} are < m_s. So B_s (after m_s) consists of elements < m_s. Ah earlier I said B2 element 4 > m1=3 but B2 is after m2=5, so B2 < m2=5, but can be > m1. So B_s < m_s, not necessarily < m_{s-1}. For s=2, B2 < m2 but may be > m1. Thus in F(S), after m1 comes B2. Current max is m1. Elements in B2 may be > m1, so some can be records. Then m2 is record. So records of F(S) include:
- records of B1 (threshold 0)
- m1
- records of B2 that are > m1
- m2
- records of B3 that are > m2
- m3
...
- records of Bt that are > m_{t-1}
- mt
But since B_s < m_s, and m_s > m_{s-1}, records of B_s greater than m_{s-1} are just the LTR maxima of B_s after starting with max m_{s-1}. This is complicated but maybe can be represented recursively.

Notice B_s is a block of elements all < m_s. The records of B_s greater than m_{s-1} are the "prefix maxima" of B_s that exceed m_{s-1}. Since m_{s-1} is the previous record. This is exactly the number of records in the concatenation [m_{s-1}] + B_s minus 1? Because m_{s-1} is a record, then records in B_s greater than m_{s-1}. So g(F(S)) = r(B1) + 1 + (r([m1]+B2)-1) + 1 + (r([m2]+B3)-1) + ... + 1? Let's define r(X) = #LTR maxima in sequence X. For B1, no preceding record, records = r(B1). Then m1 record +1. For B2 after m1, records in B2 greater than m1 = r([m1]+B2) - 1 (since m1 is first record). Then m2 record +1. So:
g = r(B1) + 1 + (r([m1]+B2)-1) + 1 + (r([m2]+B3)-1) + ... + (r([m_{t-1}]+Bt)-1) + 1? Wait last mt record +1. For t=2: g = r(B1)+1 + (r([m1]+B2)-1) +1 = r(B1)+r([m1]+B2)+1? Example [3,1,5,4,6,2]: t=3. B1=[1], r=1. m1=3. B2=[4], r([3]+[4])=2 (3,4), minus1=1. m2=5. B3=[2], r([5]+[2])=1, minus1=0. m3=6. g=1+1+1+1+0+1=5. F records count 5 (1,3,4,5,6). Yes.
This seems messy.

Maybe there is an alternative: Since operation k swaps = k - r(prefix), and r(prefix) can be maintained by a segment tree if we know for each position whether it is a record. But after operation, positions change.

Let's think about the sequence as a permutation and operation F on prefix. The transformation F(S)=B1 m1 B2 m2... is equivalent to taking the sequence of "records" and "blocks" and swapping adjacent pairs. This is exactly one step of bubble sort on the sequence of "record indicators"? If we label each element as R (record) or N (non-record), F transforms the pattern R N R N ...? Actually S = R B1 R B2 ... R Bt, where B_s are sequences of N's. F = B1 R B2 R ... Bt R. So at the level of R/N labels, it is a cyclic shift of the blocks: each R moves right after the following N-block. This is like applying a stable partition that moves all R's one block to the right. If we ignore values within blocks, the labels undergo a rotation: the first block (N's) moves before first R, etc. This is exactly the same as moving each R one position to the right in the label sequence? Let's compress each block as a single item? Since blocks can have multiple N's, but within a block, all elements are N and stay together as a block (relative order preserved). The transformation swaps adjacent "items" where items are either a single R or a block of N's: [R1][B1][R2][B2]... -> [B1][R1][B2][R2]...
This is like applying adjacent swaps between each R and the following N-block. The number of swaps = total N's = k-t.

If we consider the sequence of items (records and blocks), F is a "bubble" of records to the right by one block. Repeated F will eventually have all N-blocks before all R's? Actually sorted increasing has all elements as R, no N-blocks. Wait in sorted increasing, every element is a record, blocks empty, so F identity. In reverse sorted, first element R, all others one N-block, F -> N-block + R. Then sequence [N-block] has its own records etc. So eventually sorted.

Maybe we can maintain the permutation as a list of "runs" where each run is a maximal block of non-records? But records change after each operation.

Let's search for another angle: The operation F on prefix k is exactly one pass of bubble sort on that prefix. The number of swaps in one pass is k - r, where r is number of left-to-right maxima. There is a known formula: After one bubble sort pass on a prefix, the number of left-to-right maxima of the new prefix is equal to something like number of "descents"? Maybe we can update r efficiently using local changes? Since F only swaps records with blocks, maybe the number of records in the prefix after F can be computed from the old sequence's "record tree".

Let's examine the effect of F on the number of records for the whole prefix. Let r_old = t. What is r_new = g? Can we express r_new in terms of values? Example [3,1,5,4,6,2]: r_old=3, r_new=5. [3,2,4,1]: r_old=2, r_new=3. [4,1,3,2]: r_old=1, r_new=2? F -> [1,3,2,4], records 1,3,4 =>3? Wait records: 1,3,4 =3. r_old=1, r_new=3. [3,2,1,4]: r_old=2, r_new=3? F -> [2,1,3,4], records 2,3,4 =3. It seems r_new often > r_old. In fact F tends to increase records. Is r_new = number of elements that are not? Let's compute r_new = r(F(S)). Since F(S) = B1 m1 B2 m2... The records of F(S) include all old records m1..mt? Yes each m_s is a record in F(S) because m_s > all elements in B_s (which are before it) and > m_{s-1}? Wait m_s is after B_s? In F(S), m_s appears after B_s and before B_{s+1}? Actually F = B1 m1 B2 m2 ... Bt mt. For m_s, the elements before it are B1,m1,B2,m2,...,B_s. Are all these < m_s? B1 < m1 < m_s; m1 < m_s; B2 < m2 < m_s; ... B_s < m_s. Yes, so m_s is a record. Thus all old records remain records after F. Additionally, some elements in blocks may become records if they are greater than the previous old record. Specifically, an element in B_s becomes a record if it is greater than m_{s-1} (the previous record before it in F). Since B_s elements are < m_s but may be > m_{s-1}. So r_new = t + number of elements in blocks B_s (s>=2? and B1?) that are > m_{s-1}. For B1, before it no previous record, so all LTR maxima of B1 become records; count r(B1). For s>=2, count elements in B_s that are > m_{s-1} and form records within B_s relative to threshold m_{s-1}. But since B_s is a block after m_s, all elements in B_s are < m_s. The sequence B_s may have its own records; those greater than m_{s-1} become records. This count is r([m_{s-1}] + B_s) - 1. Not simple.

Maybe we can maintain the permutation in a data structure that supports F by "rotating" records and blocks. Since F is a deterministic transformation on the prefix, perhaps we can apply it lazily using a balanced binary tree where each node stores:
- The sequence as a Cartesian tree?
- The number of records in the prefix?
- Lazy tag representing that F has been applied some number of times?

If we apply F to a prefix multiple times, it's just multiple bubble passes. There may be a way to apply c passes to a prefix in O(log N) using the "patience sorting" / "inversion table" representation.

Let's consider the effect of c bubble sort passes on a sequence. Standard bubble sort: after c passes, each element moves left by at most c positions; larger elements move right. The resulting sequence after c passes can be described by: For each element, its position after c passes is max(initial_pos - c, something)? Let's recall.

In bubble sort (full array, left-to-right passes), after one pass, each element moves left by at most 1, and if it moves left, it's because a larger element passed it. After c passes, an element has moved left by at most c positions. More precisely, the position of value v after c passes is max(initial_pos - c, 1 + number of larger elements to its left?)? There is a known formula: After t passes of bubble sort on a permutation, the position of element x is:
pos_t(x) = max( initial_pos(x) - t, 1 + #{y > x | initial_pos(y) < initial_pos(x)} )? Let's test.
For reverse sorted [5,4,3,2,1], after 1 pass [4,3,2,1,5].
Value 4 initial pos2. Formula max(2-1=1, 1 + #{y>4 left of 4} = 1+1=2) =2. Actual pos1? Wait after first pass, 4 at pos1. So formula wrong. Maybe position = max(initial_pos - t, 1 + #{y > x | initial_pos(y) < initial_pos(x) - t?}). Let's derive.

In bubble sort, each pass moves the maximum not yet fixed to the right. The element x moves left when a larger element from its left passes over it. In one pass, a larger element can pass over x if it is immediately to the left and is a "record" moving right. Over t passes, x moves left by at most t, but only until it reaches the position just after all larger elements that were originally to its left? Actually larger elements to the left of x will eventually pass x (if they are not blocked by even larger). In sorted order, x should be after all smaller and before all larger. The number of larger elements initially to the left of x is L_x = #{y > x : pos0(y) < pos0(x)}. These larger elements must cross x to reach the right. Each pass, at most one larger element can cross x? In reverse sorted, x=4, larger to left: 5 (L=1). After first pass, 5 crossed 4, so 4 moved left to pos1. After second pass, 4 moves right? Actually after first pass [4,3,2,1,5], 4 at pos1. Second pass T5: max 5 at end, T4 on [4,3,2,1]: 4 moves right to pos4? Wait T4 on [4,3,2,1] -> [3,2,1,4], so 4 moves right from pos1 to pos4. So 4's position after 2 passes is 4. Formula not monotonic left.

So bubble sort positions are not simply initial_pos - t.

But maybe for our operations with increasing k, we are not doing full passes only. Hmm.

Let's revisit the problem constraints and possible known solution. N,M 2e5. Operation k on prefix. A non-decreasing. Need inversion count after each. The transformation F(S)=B1 m1 B2 m2... and swaps=k-records. This is very elegant. Perhaps the intended solution uses a segment tree over values to maintain "records" and a Fenwick for inversion count.

Let's think about the number of LTR maxima in a prefix. For a sequence, an element is a LTR maximum iff its value is greater than all previous elements in the prefix. Equivalently, if we consider the values in order, the LTR maxima are the elements that are "visible from the left" in a skyline. Operation F on prefix k moves each visible element right after the next invisible block. This is exactly the "pop" operation in a stack? The LTR maxima can be maintained by a monotonic stack of increasing values. The blocks are the elements popped? Let's see.

Given a sequence, we can compute LTR maxima by scanning and maintaining current max. The records are values that exceed current max. The blocks are the elements that do not. The transformation F moves each record after the following non-record block. This is similar to taking the sequence and applying a "stable partition" where records are moved to the right of the following non-records. If we represent the sequence as a linked list of elements, F on prefix k can be performed by:
- Traverse the prefix, identify records and blocks.
- For each record, move it after the following block.
This is like one pass of bubble sort. Could use a linked list and process each element? Total elements moved could be O(N^2) if many operations, but maybe each operation's number of records? Need aggregate.

Let's examine repeated F on the same prefix. This is bubble sort. The number of swaps in each pass can be computed by a known formula: For pass t, swaps = number of elements whose "bubble sort distance" > t? Specifically, in bubble sort, each inversion pair (a,b) with a>b and pos[a]<pos[b] swaps at a certain pass equal to something like the number of larger elements before a? The total swaps in pass t equals the number of inversions where the smaller element's "rank" condition. There is a known way to compute inversion count after each pass of bubble sort using a Binary Indexed Tree and the "inversion table": Let inv[i] = number of elements greater than P_i to its left? Or number of smaller to right? For bubble sort, after each pass, the inversion count decreases by the number of elements that are "not fixed". Actually standard bubble sort pass count can be simulated by:
- For each element, define d_i = number of greater elements before it (inversion count contribution as right element). In one pass, each element with d_i > 0 moves left by 1 and d_i decreases by 1? Let's check.

Consider inversion table: For each position i, let L_i = #{j < i : P_j > P_i} (number of larger elements to the left). This is the "inversion vector" of the element. In bubble sort, when a larger element passes over a smaller element, the smaller element moves left by one and its L_i decreases by one? Actually if a larger element from left swaps with smaller, the smaller moves left, so the number of larger elements to its left decreases by 1 (the one that passed). Thus after each pass, each element that has L_i > 0 may move left by one and L_i decreases by 1? But an element can move left at most one per pass, so L_i decreases by at most 1 per pass. The total swaps in a pass equals the number of elements with L_i > 0? Let's test reverse sorted [5,4,3,2,1]. L_i for each: 4 has L=1, 3 L=2, 2 L=3, 1 L=4, 5 L=0. First pass swaps = 4, number of elements with L_i>0 =4. After first pass [4,3,2,1,5], L_i: 4 at pos1 L=0, 3 L=1, 2 L=2, 1 L=3, 5 L=0. Second pass swaps? T5 on [4,3,2,1,5]: max 5 at end, T4 on [4,3,2,1] swaps 3 (4 with 3,2,1? Actually T4 on [4,3,2,1] does 3 swaps). Number of elements with L_i>0 in current array = 3 (3,2,1). Yes. So swaps in a full bubble pass equals number of elements that are not in their final position? More precisely, number of elements with at least one larger element to their left, i.e., not left-to-right minima? Wait L_i>0 means there is some larger element before it, so P_i is not a left-to-right maximum? Actually LTR maximum has no larger before it, so L_i=0. Non-record elements have L_i>0. Thus swaps in a full pass = number of non-LTR-maxima = k - records. This matches! And after each full pass, each non-record element's L_i decreases by 1? Let's check reverse sorted: 3 L=2 after first pass L=1, after second L=0. Yes. So for full array bubble sort, the number of swaps in pass t is count of elements with L_i >= t? Because each pass decrements L_i for elements with L_i>0 by 1. After t passes, an element has moved left min(L_i, t) positions. The inversion count after t passes = sum_i max(0, L_i - t)? Let's test reverse sorted N=5: L = [0,1,2,3,4]. Initial inv=10. After 1 pass inv=6 = sum max(0,L-1)=0+0+1+2+3=6. After 2 inv=3 =0+0+0+1+2=3. After 3 inv=1, after 4 inv=0. Yes! This is a known formula: bubble sort passes reduce each element's inversion count (number of larger elements to its left) by 1 per pass, until zero. The total inversion count after t full passes is sum_i max(0, L_i - t), where L_i is initial number of larger elements to the left of P_i. Great.

Now, our operations are not necessarily full passes; they are passes on prefixes of length k. But maybe we can generalize using the same idea: For each element, define L_i = number of larger elements to its left in the current array? Operation k only affects prefix k; it decrements L_i for elements in the prefix that are non-records? Actually in a prefix pass, an element in the prefix with L_i>0 (within the whole array? larger elements to its left anywhere) moves left by 1 if it is not a LTR maximum in the prefix. But if the larger element to its left is outside the prefix (position >k? impossible, left of i within prefix if i<=k; larger elements to its left are also in prefix). So L_i within prefix equals global L_i for i<=k? Since elements before i are within prefix. Yes for positions <=k, L_i (global) = number of larger elements to the left in the whole array, all of which are also in prefix. Thus an element at position i<=k is a LTR maximum in the prefix iff L_i=0. Operation k swaps = number of elements in prefix with L_i>0 = k - records. After operation, for each element in prefix with L_i>0, does L_i decrease by 1? Let's test with prefix pass not full. Example [3,2,4,1,6,5], op4. Initial L_i (larger left):
pos1 3:0
pos2 2:1 (3)
pos3 4:0
pos4 1:3 (3,2,4)
pos5 6:0
pos6 5:1 (6)
Operation 4 swaps 2, so L for pos2? After op4 array [2,3,1,4,6,5]. Compute L:
pos1 2:0
pos2 3:0
pos3 1:2 (2,3)
pos4 4:0
pos5 6:0
pos6 5:1
Initial L in prefix [0,1,0,3]. After op: [0,0,2,0]. Elements with L>0: pos2 L 1->0, pos4 L 3->2? Wait pos4 value 1 moved from pos4 to pos3? Actually value 1 at pos4 moved left to pos3; its L decreased from 3 to 2 (lost 4? no, larger elements before it now 2,3; 4 moved to right, so L decreased by1). Value 2 moved left? pos2 value2 moved left to pos1, L 1->0. So each element in prefix with L>0 decreased by 1? pos4 3->2 yes. What about value 3 at pos1 moved right to pos2, L remains 0. Value 4 moved right, L remains 0. So indeed operation k decrements L_i by 1 for every element in the prefix that had L_i>0 before the operation, and leaves L_i=0 unchanged. Does it affect elements outside prefix? No, positions >k unchanged, but their L_i may change? Elements outside prefix have larger elements to their left possibly including elements in prefix whose relative order changed. Does L_i for outside elements change? Since the set of elements before an outside position is the same (all prefix elements plus earlier outside), but their order changes. The number of larger elements before a given outside element might change if a larger element moves from after it? Wait outside position >k: all prefix elements are before it. Operation k permutes prefix elements but does not change the set of elements before position >k, only their order. For an outside element x, L_x = number of elements before x that are > x. Since the set of elements before x is unchanged (all positions <=k plus positions k+1..x-1), the count of larger elements before x is unchanged! Because it only depends on which elements are before x, not their order. Thus L_i for i>k remains same. Great! This is crucial.

So we can maintain for each element (by value) its L = number of larger elements currently before it. Initially, for value v at position pos, L_v = number of elements before pos with value > v. This is the standard inversion table. Operation k:
- It affects only elements currently in positions 1..k.
- For each such element with L_v > 0, L_v decreases by 1.
- For elements with L_v = 0, L_v stays 0.
- Elements outside prefix unchanged.
The number of swaps in operation k is exactly the number of elements in prefix with L_v > 0. After operation, those L_v are decremented by 1. The inversion count after operation = sum_v L_v (since total inversions = sum over elements of number of larger elements before them). This is a much simpler model!

Let's verify with sample. Initial P [3,2,4,1,6,5].
Compute L by value:
v=3 pos1: larger before none ->0
v=2 pos2: larger before {3}->1
v=4 pos3: none ->0
v=1 pos4: larger before {3,2,4}->3
v=6 pos5:0
v=5 pos6: larger before {6}->1
Sum=5 initial inversions? Inversions: (3,2),(3,1),(2,1),(4,1),(6,5)=5 yes.

Operation k=4: prefix positions 1..4 contain values 3(L0),2(L1),4(L0),1(L3). Elements with L>0 in prefix: 2,1 -> count2. Decrement them: L2=0, L1=2. L values: 3:0,2:0,4:0,1:2,6:0,5:1. Sum=3. Output 3. Matches.
Operation k=6: prefix all positions. L values: 3:0,2:0,4:0,1:2,6:0,5:1. Elements with L>0: 1(L2),5(L1) count2. Decrement: L1=1,L5=0. Sum=1. Output 1. Matches! Great.

Sample 2? We can trust.

This is the key insight! Let's formalize.

For each value v (1..N), define c_v = number of values greater than v that are currently to the left of v. Initially, c_v can be computed from initial permutation. Inversion number = sum c_v.

Operation k:
- Consider the set S_k of values currently located in positions 1..k.
- For every v in S_k with c_v > 0, decrement c_v by 1.
- Values not in S_k unchanged.
- Number of swaps = |{v in S_k : c_v > 0}|.
Why is this true? We argued each element in prefix with c_v>0 moves left by one and its c_v decreases by 1; elements with c_v=0 are LTR maxima and may move right but c_v remains 0. Need prove operation k exactly decrements c_v for all prefix elements with c_v>0. Let's prove via bubble pass property: In one bubble pass on a sequence, for each element x, the number of larger elements before x decreases by 1 if it was >0, else 0. Is this always true? Let's test with a random sequence and operation prefix.

Take S=[2,4,1,3], operation k=4. Initial L by position/value:
2:0, 4:0, 1:2 (2,4), 3:1 (4). Sum3.
Simulate F: records 2,4,3? Wait LTR maxima: 2(pos1),4(pos2),3(pos4)? 3>2 but <4, so not record. Actually records: 2,4. Blocks: B1 after2 before4 empty, B2 after4 before? next record none? But 3 after4 is <4, so B2=[1,3]? Let's decompose S=2, B1 empty, 4, B2=[1,3]. F = B1 2 B2 4 = [2,1,3,4]. Simulate bubble: compare 2<4 no; 4>1 swap -> [2,1,4,3]; 4>3 swap -> [2,1,3,4]. Swaps=2. Initial L: 2:0,4:0,1:2,3:1. Prefix all, elements with L>0: 1,3 count2. Decrement: 1->1,3->0. New L: 2:0,4:0,1:1,3:0. Sum1. Inversion count of [2,1,3,4] is 1. Good. L for value1 decreased from2 to1, value3 from1 to0. Value1 moved left? Initial pos3, final pos2 (left1). Value3 pos4 -> pos3 (left1). Yes.

Another example S=[3,1,4,2], op4. Initial L: 3:0,1:1,4:0,2:2 (3,4). Sum3. Simulate F: records 3,4; B1=[1], B2=[2]; F=[1,3,2,4]. Swaps=2. Decrement L for 1 (1->0), 2 (2->1). New L sum1. Inversions [1,3,2,4]: (3,2)=1. Good.

What about an element with L>0 that is a LTR maximum? Impossible: LTR maximum means no larger before, so L=0. So all L>0 elements are non-records and move left by one. Does every non-record in prefix move left by exactly one? In F(S)=B1 m1 B2 m2..., each block element moves left by one position relative to the whole sequence? Let's check if a block element can move left by more than one? In [3,1,4,2], B1=[1] pos2 -> pos1 (left1), B2=[2] pos4 -> pos3 (left1). Yes. In general, each non-record is in some block B_s; in F, B_s is placed before m_s, so every element in B_s shifts left by exactly one position (because the record m_s that was immediately before B_s moves to after B_s, and the block as a whole shifts left by one). Wait B_s was after m_s; in F, B_s is before m_s. The position of the first element of B_s decreases by 1 (since m_s moves from before B_s to after B_s). The subsequent elements also decrease by 1 because the whole block shifts left. Thus every non-record moves left by exactly one position. Therefore the number of larger elements before it decreases by exactly 1? Moving left by one position swaps it with the element immediately before it, which is a larger element? In the original sequence, the element immediately before B_s is m_s (a record, larger than all in B_s). So each non-record swaps with m_s? Wait in F, B_s moves before m_s, so each element in B_s crosses m_s. But within the block, elements don't cross each other. Thus each non-record crosses exactly one larger element (the record m_s). So its L decreases by exactly 1. What about elements in B_s that had L>1? They lose the record m_s from their left, so L--. If L was 0? Can a block element have L=0? No, because the record m_s immediately before it is larger, so L>=1. Thus all block elements have L>0. Records have L=0 and move right, L unchanged. Elements outside prefix unchanged. Therefore operation k decrements L_v by 1 for exactly the elements in prefix with L_v>0. Perfect.

This is the core invariant.

Now the problem reduces to maintaining an array c[1..N] (for each value) under operations:
- Operation k: Let S_k be the set of values currently in positions 1..k. For all v in S_k with c[v] > 0, decrement c[v] by 1.
- The number of decremented elements in this operation is the number of swaps; output initial_inv - cumulative_swaps, or equivalently sum c after update.
Need also update the set S_k (positions of values) because values move: non-records move left by one, records move right. But maybe we don't need to maintain exact positions? To know S_k for future operations, we need which values are in first k positions. Since operations are nondecreasing k, and values move within prefixes, positions change. Need maintain positions of values or a data structure for S_k.

However, maybe we can characterize S_k in terms of c values? Operation k decrements c for all values in prefix with c>0. Values with c=0 in prefix are records and move right; values with c>0 move left by one. This changes positions. We need update positions to know future prefixes.

Let's examine position dynamics in terms of c. Initially, position of value v is given. Operation k:
- For values in prefix:
  - If c[v] > 0: position decreases by 1 (moves left one).
  - If c[v] = 0: position increases by (number of consecutive non-records after it?) Actually records move right by the size of following block, not necessarily 1. But in terms of positions, a record m_s moves right by |B_s|, which is the number of elements in the following block. Those block elements all move left by 1. So positions of records can jump right by varying amounts.
Can we update positions using c? The block size following a record is the number of consecutive elements with c>0 before the next record. This is local.

But maybe we don't need exact positions if we process operations in increasing k using a different perspective: Since A is nondecreasing, operation k only affects the prefix of length k. Values outside prefix (positions >k) are unchanged. For future operations with larger k, the prefix expands to include more values. We can maintain a data structure over positions that supports:
- Given k, find all values in positions 1..k with c>0, decrement them, and update their positions (left by 1); values with c=0 in prefix move right by block sizes.
This is essentially simulating bubble sort on prefixes. Need efficient.

Let's think in terms of c values and positions. The operation F on prefix k can be described at the level of c:
- All values in prefix with c>0 have c--.
- Values with c=0 are records; they move right past the following block of c>0 values.
This is exactly the same as bubble sort but now c is like "distance to move left". Each value v needs to move left c[v] times total (until c=0). Operation k decrements c for all active values in the current prefix. A value's c decreases by 1 each time it is included in an operation while c>0. Once c=0, it becomes a record and no longer decreases; it may move right, but c stays 0.

This is reminiscent of "each value has a counter c_v; operation k decrements counters of all values in prefix with positive counter". The total number of decrements is total swaps. We need know which values are in prefix.

Can we determine whether a value v is in prefix k from its current position. Position of v changes:
- When c[v] > 0 and v is in an operation, it moves left by 1.
- When c[v] = 0 and v is in an operation, it may move right by the number of positive-counter values immediately after it (block).
So positions are dynamic.

Maybe there is a dual representation using values sorted by position. Since c[v] is the number of larger values before v. Initially, c[v] can be computed. Operation k decrements c[v] for all v in prefix with c[v]>0. This is equivalent to: For each value v, c[v] decreases by the number of operations k such that v is in prefix k at that time and c[v]>0. Since c[v] starts at some value and decreases to 0. It will be decremented exactly c[v] times over the entire process if enough operations include it. The times when it is decremented are the operations where its position <= k. Its position depends on how many times it has been decremented and how many records have passed over it.

Maybe we can process values independently? For a value v, its position at any time = initial_pos - (#times v moved left) + (#larger values that passed over v from left to right). Actually each time a larger value passes over v, v moves left by 1 (decrement c). Each time v passes over a smaller value (as a record), v moves right by 1. The net position change = (#smaller values v has passed) - (#larger values that passed v). The number of larger values that passed v is exactly initial c[v] - current c[v] (decrements). The number of smaller values v has passed is related to v being a record in operations. Hard.

But note: A value v with c[v]>0 is never a record, so it never passes smaller values; it only moves left when larger values pass it. Once c[v]=0, it becomes a record and may pass smaller values. Thus for v, before its c reaches 0, it only moves left (one per decrement). After c=0, it may move right by passing smaller values. This is like each value first moves left c[v] times, then moves right.

Could we compute the operation times when v is in prefix using a priority queue of positions? Operation k affects all values in prefix. If we maintain values sorted by position, operation k needs to decrement c for a prefix of the position order. But after decrement, positions of those values change (left by 1), and records (c=0) in the prefix move right. This is like a stable partition of the prefix by c>0.

Let's consider maintaining the sequence of values in position order. Operation k:
- Let prefix list S = values at positions 1..k.
- Partition S into records (c=0) and non-records (c>0), preserving order? Actually S = m1 B1 m2 B2...; after F, S' = B1 m1 B2 m2... This is not simply all non-records before all records; it's alternating. But at the level of individual values, each non-record moves left by one position, each record moves right by block size. The relative order among non-records is preserved; relative order among records is preserved; but records and non-records interleave.

Maybe we can maintain c values in a Fenwick tree over positions and support operation k by:
- Find all positions i<=k with c[P_i]>0. Decrement c[P_i] by 1.
- Update positions: For each such element, position--. For records in prefix, position += number of consecutive non-records after them. This is complex.

Let's look for a simpler way to compute S_k without updating positions explicitly. Since A is nondecreasing, operation k is applied in increasing k. Maybe we can process k from 2 to N and maintain a data structure over values where each value has a "deadline" or "activation" based on initial position and c.

Let's simulate the process in terms of c and positions for a small example to see patterns.

Example P=[3,2,4,1,6,5], c by value:
v:1 c=3, pos4
2 c=1, pos2
3 c=0, pos1

Operation k=4: prefix positions 1..4 values [3(c0),2(c1),4(c0),1(c3)]. Decrement c for 2,1 -> c2=0,c1=2. Positions after op: [2,3,1,4,6,5] positions: 2 pos1,3 pos2,4 pos3,1 pos4? Wait final [2,3,1,4,6,5], so value1 pos4? Initially pos4, moved left? Actually value1 moved from pos4 to pos3? Final array positions: pos1=2,pos2=3,pos3=1,pos4=4. Value1 pos3 (left1). Now, can we determine the set of values in prefix k using c and initial positions? At operation k, the prefix consists of values whose current position <= k. Current position of v = initial_pos[v] - (number of times v moved left) + (number of times v moved right). The number of times v moved left is initial_c[v] - c[v] (decrements). The number of times v moved right is the number of operations where v was a record (c=0) and had non-records after it in the prefix. This seems hard.

But maybe we can avoid positions by processing operations in increasing k and using a priority queue of "events" when a value's c becomes 0? Let's think.

Operation k decrements c for all values in prefix with c>0. This is equivalent to: For each value v, it will be decremented in an operation k iff its current position <= k. Since operations k are increasing, once a value's position becomes <= some k, it remains <= future larger k? Positions can move right, so a value might leave a prefix? Example value4 in sample after op4 at pos4; operation k=4 (same) if repeated would include it? If k=4, pos4 <=4 yes. If later k=5, pos4 <=5 yes. Values can move right but only within prefix; for a fixed k, after operation k, records may move right to positions <=k, so they remain in prefix k. For future larger k, they are still in prefix. Could a value move from position <=k to >k? Operation k only permutes within first k, so no value leaves first k. Future operations with larger k' > k may move a value to position >k but still <=k'. For a given operation k', a value that was in prefix of previous smaller operations is still in prefix k'? Since operations only permute within prefixes, and k' is nondecreasing, the set of positions <=k' is a superset of previous prefix. But a value could move from position <=k' to >k' during an operation k'? Operation k' permutes within first k', so no value leaves first k'. Therefore, once a value is in the prefix of an operation, it remains in all future prefixes (because future k >= current k and operations never move elements beyond the current prefix). Wait if future k is larger, an element in first k_old remains in first k_future? Operation k_future permutes within first k_future, so yes it stays within first k_future. It cannot move to position >k_future. Thus the set of values that have ever been in any prefix is monotonically increasing with k? Actually at operation k, the prefix of length k includes all values that were in previous prefixes plus some new values from positions k_old+1..k. Since operations never move elements out of the current prefix, the set S_k (values in first k) is nondecreasing as k increases. Let's verify: After op4 in sample, S_4={1,2,3,4}. After op6, S_6={1,2,3,4,5,6}. Yes. If k stays same, S_k unchanged (operation k permutes within first k). If k increases, new values from positions k_old+1..k are added, and old values remain. This is huge!

Let's prove: Operation k only swaps adjacent elements within positions 1..k. Therefore the set of elements in positions 1..k is invariant under operation k. For a future operation k' >= k, since it only swaps within positions 1..k', and the elements in positions 1..k are a subset of positions 1..k', they remain in positions 1..k' (though not necessarily in 1..k). Thus once an element enters the prefix of current operation, it stays in all future prefixes. So S_k is monotonically increasing with the operation's k.

Therefore, we can process operations in increasing k and maintain a set of "active" values that are currently in the prefix. When the operation k increases, we add all values that are currently at positions between old_k+1 and new_k to the active set. But positions of values may have changed due to previous operations; however, the values not yet in the prefix (positions > old_k) have not been affected by any previous operation (since previous operations had k <= old_k and only permuted within <= old_k). Is that true? If previous operations had k' <= old_k, they did not touch positions > old_k. So the relative order and positions of values outside the current prefix remain exactly as initial! Wait if k' < old_k, yes positions > old_k untouched. If k' = old_k, also positions > old_k untouched. Thus before processing an operation with new k, the values in positions old_k+1..k are exactly the initial values in those positions, unchanged. Because no operation has touched beyond old_k. This is true as long as we process k nondecreasing and old_k is the maximum k seen so far. The prefix of maximum length seen so far is the only part modified; the suffix beyond it is pristine.

Let's verify with sample: After op4, positions 5,6 remain initial [6,5]. Yes. So when moving from k=4 to k=6, we add values at initial positions 5 and 6: 6,5. Their c values are initial? They have not been decremented because they were outside previous prefixes. Yes c5=1,c6=0 unchanged. Good.

Thus the active set S_k is simply the set of values whose initial position <= current maximum k? Wait after op4, S_4={values in positions 1..4} = initial positions 1..4? Operation op4 permuted within first4, but the set is exactly initial first4 values {3,2,4,1}. Yes. After op6, S_6 = initial first6 all values. In general, after processing up to maximum K, the active set is exactly the values with initial position <= K. Because operations never change the set of elements in the prefix of maximum length seen; when K increases, the new elements are exactly those initially in positions oldK+1..K. Therefore, at any point, the set of values in the current prefix of length k (where k is the current operation's A_i) is exactly the set of values whose initial position <= k! Wait what about if previous operations had k larger? We process nondecreasing, so current k is the maximum seen so far. The prefix length k contains exactly the initial first k values as a set. Yes, because no operation with length > k has occurred, and operations with length <= k only permute within first k, preserving the set. Thus S_k is static: S_k = {P_1,...,P_k} initially. This is independent of the transformations! Excellent.

Let's confirm with repeated same k: Operation k permutes within first k, set unchanged. So S_k remains initial first k values. Therefore, for operation k, the set of values in the prefix is simply the set of values whose initial position <= k. We don't need to track positions at all for determining which values are affected! Is that true even after operations with smaller k? Yes, they only permute within first smaller prefix, which is subset of first k, so set of first k unchanged. Operations with same k also preserve set. Thus S_k = initial prefix set.

This is a massive simplification. Let's test with a counterexample: Suppose N=3, P=[2,3,1], operations k=2 then k=3.
Initial first2 set {2,3}. Op2: compare 2<3 no, array [2,3,1], set first2 {2,3}. Op3: set first3 {2,3,1}. Yes.
If op2 had swapped, set first2 still same. Good.

Therefore, operation k affects exactly the values whose initial position <= k, regardless of current positions. And for each such value, if its current c_v > 0, decrement c_v by 1. Values with initial position > k are untouched (c unchanged). This means the process is:
- Maintain c[v] for all values.
- For each operation k, for all v with initial_pos[v] <= k and c[v] > 0, decrement c[v] by 1.
- The number of swaps is the count of such v.

Is it really that simple? Let's verify with sample operation k=4: initial_pos<=4 values {3,2,4,1}. c before: 3:0,2:1,4:0,1:3. Count c>0 =2 (2,1), decrement -> c2=0,c1=2. Matches.
Operation k=6: initial_pos<=6 all values. c before: 1:2,2:0,3:0,4:0,5:1,6:0. Count c>0 =2 (1,5), decrement -> c1=1,c5=0. Matches.

Let's test another random sequence with operations not full. N=4, P=[2,4,1,3]. Initial c:
pos1 2:0
pos2 4:0
pos3 1:2 (2,4)
pos4 3:1 (4)
c:1=2,2=0,3=1,4=0.
Operation k=3: initial_pos<=3 values {2,4,1}. c>0: 1 (c=2) only. Decrement c1=1. Swaps=1. Simulate op3 on [2,4,1]: compare 2<4 no; 4>1 swap -> [2,1,4,3]. Inversions: (2,1) only? Array [2,1,4,3] inversions (2,1),(4,3)=2. Initial inv=3. Swaps=1, inv=2. c sum: 1:1,3:1 total2. Good.
Operation k=4: initial_pos<=4 all. c>0: 1(c1),3(c1) count2. Decrement -> c1=0,c3=0. Swaps=2, inv=0. Simulate op4 on [2,1,4,3]: compare 2>1 swap -> [1,2,4,3]; compare 2<4; compare 4>3 swap -> [1,2,3,4]. Swaps=2. Good.

This is almost too easy. Let's prove the key invariant rigorously.

Define for each value x:
- pos0(x) = initial position.
- c_x(t) = number of values greater than x that are currently to the left of x after some operations.
Initially c_x = # {y > x : pos0(y) < pos0(x)}.

Consider an operation k. Let S = {x : pos0(x) <= k}. We claim:
1. The set of values in positions 1..k is exactly S before and after the operation.
Proof: Initially before any operation with max length K, the set in first K is initial first K. Operations with length <=K only swap within first K, preserving the set. Since A nondecreasing, when operation k is performed, all previous operations had length <=k, so the set in first k is initial first k. The operation itself preserves it.
2. During operation k, every value x in S with c_x>0 moves left by exactly one position, and its c_x decreases by 1; every value with c_x=0 (in S) moves right or stays, c_x unchanged; values not in S unaffected.
Need prove using bubble pass decomposition. In one bubble pass on the prefix sequence, the elements with c_x=0 are exactly the left-to-right maxima of the prefix? Let's verify: For an element x in the prefix, c_x is the number of larger elements before it in the entire array. Since all elements before x are also in the prefix (because x in prefix), c_x>0 iff there is a larger element before it in the prefix, i.e., x is not a left-to-right maximum. c_x=0 iff x is a left-to-right maximum. Thus records = c=0. The bubble pass transformation F(S)=B1 m1 B2 m2... moves each non-record (c>0) left by one position, and each record (c=0) right by block size. Moving left by one across the preceding record (which is larger) reduces the number of larger elements to its left by exactly 1, so c_x decreases by 1. Records have no larger elements before, moving right doesn't change c_x (still 0). Values outside prefix: their set of preceding elements unchanged? Actually for a value outside prefix (position >k), all prefix elements are before it. Operation k permutes prefix elements but the set of elements before it remains the same (all positions <=k plus maybe positions between k+1 and its position). The count of larger elements before it depends only on the set, not order, so c unchanged. Good.
Thus operation k is exactly: decrement c_x by 1 for all x with pos0(x)<=k and c_x>0.

This is the entire solution! Then we just need to process M operations efficiently.

We have N,M up to 2e5. For each operation k, we need to decrement c_v by 1 for all values v with initial_pos[v] <= k and c_v > 0, and count how many such decrements. Since A is nondecreasing, the set of eligible values by initial position expands as k increases. We can maintain a data structure over values v (1..N) with:
- c_v current value (nonnegative integer).
- pos0[v] initial position.
Operation k: consider all v with pos0[v] <= k and c_v > 0. Decrement c_v by 1. Count = number of such v.

This is a range update over values sorted by initial position: For all indices i <= k (where i is initial position), if c[P_i] > 0, decrement c[P_i] by 1. We need count of positive c among first k positions, and decrement them all by 1. Since c values can be large (up to N), and each value may be decremented many times (up to initial c), total decrements over all operations can be O(N^2) (e.g., reverse sorted with full passes? Wait initial c sum can be O(N^2), but operations M up to 2e5; total decrements could be O(N^2) if M large? But each operation decrements many values; total decrements over all operations equals total swaps, which can be O(N^2) (reverse sorted with M=N gives O(N^2)). We cannot iterate over each decremented value per operation. Need aggregate.

We need support: Given an array c[1..N] (indexed by initial position i), operation k:
- For i=1..k, if c[i] > 0: c[i] -= 1, and count++.
- Values with c[i]=0 unchanged.
This is exactly: decrement all positive elements in prefix by 1, count how many were positive. Need do for M up to 2e5, N 2e5. This is a classic data structure problem: range "decrement positive elements by 1" and query count of decremented elements. Since each decrement reduces c[i] by 1, and c[i] can be up to N. Total sum of c is initial inversions O(N^2), but each element's c decreases to 0; total number of decrements per element is c[i]. Sum c[i] can be O(N^2) ~ 2e10, cannot process individually. Need a data structure that can decrement all positive values in a prefix by 1 in sublinear time, perhaps using segment tree with lazy "subtract 1 from positive" and count positives.

But note operations k are nondecreasing, so the prefix for updates is expanding. We process k increasing. We need apply operation k to prefix [1..k] of the initial-position-indexed array c. Since k is nondecreasing, the update range is always a prefix that only grows. This is important. We can process by adding new elements as k increases, and maintain some aggregate over the active prefix.

Let's re-index by initial position i. Let a_i = c_{P_i} (initial c for value at initial position i). Operation k: for i=1..k, if a_i > 0, a_i--. Count decrements. Since k increases, when k increases, new positions i become included in future operations. Previously included positions continue to be decremented in every operation where they are positive.

This is reminiscent of maintaining a multiset of positive "heights" a_i. Each operation with current k decrements all positive heights among first k by 1. Since k is nondecreasing, we can think of time t = number of operations processed. For each position i, it is "active" from the first operation with k >= i. Once active, its height a_i decreases by 1 in every subsequent operation until it reaches 0. Therefore, for a position i, the number of decrements it undergoes up to time t is:
d_i(t) = min( a_i, number of operations among first t with k >= i? Wait it is active for operations with k >= i. Since k nondecreasing, let t_i be the first operation index such that A_{t_i} >= i. Then position i is active for operations t_i, t_i+1, ..., t. So the number of operations it has been active for is max(0, t - t_i + 1). Its height after t operations is a_i' = max(0, a_i - (t - t_i + 1)). The number of decrements for i at operation t (if active and positive) is 1 if a_i' before operation >0, else 0. Total swaps up to t = sum_i min(a_i, max(0, t - t_i + 1)). Is that correct? Let's test sample.

Initial a by position i: P=[3,2,4,1,6,5].
a_1 for value3 =0
a_2 value2 =1
a_3 value4 =0
a_4 value1 =3
a_5 value6 =0
a_6 value5 =1
A=[4,6].
For each position i, t_i = first op with A>=i:
i=1: t=1 (4>=1)
i=2: t=1
i=3: t=1
i=4: t=1
i=5: t=2 (6>=5)
i=6: t=2
After t=1: d_i = min(a_i, 1-1+1=1) for active i<=4: a: [0,1,0,3] -> d [0,1,0,1] total2? But actual swaps op4=2. Wait d_i = min(a_i, operations active) = min(1,1)=1 for i2, min(3,1)=1 for i4, total2. Good.
After t=2: active durations: i1-4 active 2 ops, i5-6 active 1 op. d_i = min(a_i, duration):
i1 min(0,2)=0
i2 min(1,2)=1
i3 0
i4 min(3,2)=2
i5 min(0,1)=0
i6 min(1,1)=1
Total d=4? Total swaps after two ops = op4 2 + op6 2 =4. Initial inv 5, final inv1, total swaps4. Yes. But wait i4 value1 c after op4 was2, after op6 became1, so d=2. Good.

So total swaps after processing first t operations is:
S(t) = sum_{i=1}^N min( a_i, max(0, t - t_i + 1) )
where t_i = min index j such that A_j >= i (if none, then i never active; but A_M <= N and eventually maybe; if no operation >=i, duration 0). Since A nondecreasing, t_i is well-defined for i <= A_M; for i > A_M, never active, contribution 0.

Then inversion count after t = initial_inv - S(t).

This is a huge simplification! We don't need to simulate decrements individually; we can compute cumulative swaps using per-position activation time and initial a_i.

Let's verify with another example. N=4, P=[2,4,1,3], a=[0,0,2,1] (by initial pos: value2 c0, value4 c0, value1 c2, value3 c1). A=[3,4].
t_i:
i1: first A>=1 is op1 ->1
i2: op1 ->1
i3: op1 ->1 (A1=3)
i4: op2 ->2 (A2=4)
After t=1: durations active: i1-3 =1, i4=0. S= min(0,1)+min(0,1)+min(2,1)+0=1. Actual op3 swaps1. Good.
After t=2: durations i1-3=2, i4=1. S=0+0+min(2,2)=2 + min(1,1)=1 total3. Initial inv3, final0. Actual op4 swaps2, total3. Good.

Thus the problem reduces to computing for each query t (1..M):
S(t) = sum_i min(a_i, max(0, t - t_i + 1)).
Where a_i = initial c for value at initial position i, i.e., number of larger elements before it in initial permutation.
t_i = first operation index with A_{t_i} >= i. Since A nondecreasing, we can compute t_i for all i by scanning A. For i > A_M, t_i = infinity (never active), contribution 0.

Then answer_t = total_inv - S(t).

Need output after each operation i=1..M. We can compute S(t) for all t efficiently.

Let's define for each position i:
- a_i >=0.
- l_i = t_i (activation operation index, 1-based). If i > A_M, l_i = M+1 maybe.
Contribution to S(t) for t >= l_i is min(a_i, t - l_i + 1). For t < l_i, 0.
This is a piecewise linear function: 0 for t < l_i, then increases by 1 per t until reaching a_i at t = l_i + a_i - 1, then constant a_i.
So S(t) = sum_i f_i(t), where f_i(t) = 0 if t < l_i; = t - l_i + 1 if l_i <= t < l_i + a_i; = a_i if t >= l_i + a_i. (If a_i=0, f_i=0 always.)

We need evaluate S(t) for t=1..M. M up to 2e5, N up to 2e5. We can compute S(t) incrementally using difference arrays.

For each i, f_i(t) contributes:
- +1 to S(t) for each t from l_i to l_i + a_i - 1 (inclusive), because during the increasing phase, f_i increases by 1 each step. Actually f_i(t) = t - l_i + 1, so the increment in S from t-1 to t is 1 for each i active and not yet saturated. Thus total increment ΔS(t) = S(t) - S(t-1) = number of i with l_i <= t <= l_i + a_i - 1? Let's check: f_i(t) - f_i(t-1) = 1 if l_i <= t <= l_i + a_i - 1, else 0. For t < l_i, 0. For t >= l_i + a_i, constant, 0. So yes.
Therefore S(t) = sum_{s=1}^t Δ(s), where Δ(s) = count of i such that l_i <= s <= l_i + a_i - 1.

This is easy: For each i, it contributes +1 to Δ(s) for s in interval [l_i, l_i + a_i - 1]. Then we can build a difference array over operation indices 1..M:
diff[l_i] += 1
if l_i + a_i <= M: diff[l_i + a_i] -= 1  (since interval inclusive ends at l_i+a_i-1; subtract at end+1)
Then prefix sum gives Δ(s), and prefix sum of Δ gives S(t).

Let's test sample1:
a by pos: [0,1,0,3,0,1].
A=[4,6], M=2.
l_i first op >=i:
i1: A1=4>=1 ->1
i2:1
i3:1
i4:1
i5: A2=6>=5 ->2
i6:2
For i with a>0:
i2: l=1,a=1 interval [1,1] -> diff[1]+=1, diff[2]-=1
i4: l=1,a=3 interval [1,3] but M=2 -> diff[1]+=1, diff[3]-=1 (beyond M ignore)
i6: l=2,a=1 interval [2,2] -> diff[2]+=1, diff[3]-=1
diff: [0,0,0]; after i2: diff1=1,diff2=-1. i4: diff1=2,diff3=-1. i6: diff2=0? diff2=-1+1=0. Prefix Δ: s1=2, s2=2. S(1)=2, S(2)=4. Matches.

Sample2? We can trust.

Now we need compute a_i = initial c for value at initial position i. Given P permutation. For each position i from 1..N, value v=P_i. a_i = number of previous elements greater than v. This is the standard inversion contribution. We can compute with Fenwick tree over values:
Initialize BIT empty. Iterate i=1..N:
- a_i = i-1 - BIT.sum(P_i) (number of elements before i with value > P_i) = (i-1) - (# previous values <= P_i). Since permutation, # previous values < P_i = BIT.sum(P_i-1), but BIT.sum(P_i) includes P_i if already inserted? We insert after query, so BIT.sum(P_i) = # previous values <= P_i = # previous values < P_i. So a_i = (i-1) - BIT.sum(P_i).
- Add P_i to BIT.
Total_inv = sum a_i.
Alternatively, a_i = number of larger before.

Let's verify sample1:
i1 v3: BIT sum3=0, a=0.
i2 v2: sum2=0, a=1.
i3 v4: sum4=1 (3), a=1? Wait (i-1)=2, sum4=1, a=1. But earlier a_3 for value4 was 0! Let's recalc: P=[3,2,4,1,6,5]. At i=3, previous elements: 3,2. Value4 is greater than both, so number of larger before =0. Formula (i-1) - BIT.sum(P_i) where BIT.sum(P_i) counts previous elements <= P_i. Previous <=4 are 3 and2, count2. (i-1)=2, a=0. I mistakenly said sum4=1; after inserting 3 and2, BIT.sum(4)=2. So a=0. Good.
i4 v1: previous <=1 count0, a=3.
i5 v6: previous <=6 count4, a=0.
i6 v5: previous <=5 count4? Previous values 3,2,4,1,6; <=5 are 3,2,4,1 =4, a=5-1? i-1=5, a=1. Good.

So a_i = (i-1) - bit.sum(P_i).

Now l_i = first operation index t such that A_t >= i. Since A nondecreasing length M. We can compute by two-pointer:
For i from 1 to N, maintain pointer t increasing while t<=M and A_t < i. Then l_i = t if t<=M else M+1 (never active). Since A_t >=2, for i=1 l=1 always.
Actually if i=1, A_t>=2>=1, l=1.
We can compute l_i array for all i.

Then for each i with a_i > 0 and l_i <= M:
start = l_i
end = l_i + a_i - 1
if end > M: end = M (we only need up to M; interval beyond M irrelevant)
if start <= end:
  diff[start] += 1
  if end+1 <= M: diff[end+1] -= 1

Then compute Δ_s = prefix sum of diff over s=1..M.
Then S_t cumulative: cur += Δ_s; answer_t = total_inv - cur.

Let's test with a case where a_i large and l_i late. Suppose N=3, P=[3,2,1], a: i1 v3 a0; i2 v2 a1; i3 v1 a2. total_inv=3. A=[3] M=1.
l_i: i1 l=1, i2 l=1, i3 l=1.
i2 a1 interval [1,1] diff1+1,diff2-1.
i3 a2 interval [1,2] but M=1 -> [1,1] diff1+1.
diff1=2, diff2=-1. Δ1=2. S1=2, ans=1. Simulate op3 on [3,2,1]: compare 3>2 swap [2,3,1]; compare 3>1 swap [2,1,3]. Swaps2, inv1. Good.

If A=[2,3] M=2. l_i: i1 l1, i2 l1 (A1=2), i3 l2 (A2=3).
a: i2=1 interval [1,1]; i3=2 interval [2,3] -> [2,2] (M=2).
diff: i2 diff1+1,diff2-1; i3 diff2+1,diff3-1. diff1=1,diff2=0. Δ1=1, Δ2=1. S1=1, ans2; S2=2, ans1. Simulate: op2 on [3,2,1]: compare 3>2 swap -> [2,3,1], swaps1, inv2. op3 on [2,3,1]: compare 2<3, 3>1 swap -> [2,1,3], swaps1, total2, inv1. Good.

What about a value with a_i=0: no interval, never contributes. It may become a record and move right but no swaps involving it as smaller? Actually a_i=0 means no larger before initially; it may later have larger before? Wait c_v counts larger before current position. Could a value with c=0 later get a larger element before it? Larger elements can move right, but can they move from right to left of a smaller value? In bubble sort, larger elements move right, smaller move left. A larger element initially to the right of a smaller value will never move to its left (because that would be moving left across smaller, which doesn't happen). Thus if no larger before initially, no larger will ever be before it. So c remains 0. Good.

Now, is total_inv - S(t) always nonnegative? S(t) <= total_inv because each a_i contributes at most a_i, sum total_inv. Yes.

Let's prove the interval formula more rigorously:
For each initial position i, let a_i be initial c. It is active from operation l_i onward. In each operation t >= l_i, if its current c>0, it decrements by 1. Since it starts at a_i and decrements once per operation, after d operations its c = max(0, a_i - d). It contributes 1 to swaps for each of the first a_i active operations. Thus its contribution to total swaps up to operation t is min(a_i, number of active operations up to t) = min(a_i, max(0, t - l_i + 1)). This is exactly the interval contribution.

This is almost the final solution. Need be careful: a_i is indexed by initial position, not by value. Operation k affects values with initial position <= k, i.e., positions 1..k. So yes.

Let's test with a more complex scenario where A has duplicates and k not increasing strictly. Suppose N=3, P=[2,1,3], a: i1 v2 a0; i2 v1 a1; i3 v3 a0. total_inv=1. A=[2,2,3] M=3.
l_i: i1 l1, i2 l1, i3 l3.
i2 a1 interval [1,1]. diff1+1,diff2-1. Δ1=1, Δ2=0, Δ3=0. S:1,1,1. Answers:0,0,0? Simulate:
Initial [2,1,3], inv1.
op2: compare 2>1 swap -> [1,2,3], inv0. swaps1.
op2 again: prefix [1,2] sorted, no swaps. inv0.
op3: prefix [1,2,3] sorted, no swaps. inv0. Matches.

What about A=[3,3] on same P. l_i: i1 l1, i2 l1, i3 l1. i2 a1 interval [1,1]. S1=1,S2=1. Simulate op3 first: [2,1,3] -> compare 2>1 swap [1,2,3], compare 2<3, inv0. op3 again no swaps. Good.

What about value with a_i=2 and l_i=1, M=1: interval [1,1] contributes 1, not 2, because only one operation. Good.

Now, compute a_i with BIT. N up to 2e5, O(N log N). M up to 2e5. Difference array O(N+M). Total O(N log N + N + M). Perfect.

Let's double-check the proof that S_k set is initial first k even after operations with same k and smaller k. We already did. More formal:
Let K be the maximum A_j among operations processed so far. No operation has ever touched positions >K. Therefore positions >K remain exactly initial. Operations with length <=K only permute the elements within positions 1..K, so the set of elements in positions 1..K is always the initial set of first K. When current operation has k=K, the prefix set is initial first k. Since A nondecreasing, current k is the maximum so far. Thus S_k = initial first k. This is independent of the actual permutation within prefix. Great.

Now, one subtlety: Operation k affects values in positions 1..k at the start of the operation. We claim the set is initial first k. Yes.

Another subtlety: c_v = number of larger elements before v in the current array. We need show operation k decrements c_v for all v in S_k with c_v>0. We used bubble pass property. Let's prove with the block decomposition:
In the current prefix sequence, elements with c_v=0 are exactly LTR maxima. Let them be m_1,...,m_t. The elements between m_s and m_{s+1} (block B_s) all have c>0 because m_s is a larger element before them. The operation transforms S to B1 m1 B2 m2 ... Bt mt. Thus every element in a block moves left by one position, crossing the record m_s that was immediately before it. Since m_s is larger than all elements in B_s, the number of larger elements before each block element decreases by exactly 1. Elements with c=0 (records) may move right but no larger element is before them, so c stays 0. Elements outside prefix: the set of elements before them is unchanged, so c unchanged. This is rigorous.

Now, compute a_i = initial c for value at initial position i. But note c_v is associated with value, not position. Operation k decrements values in initial first k set. In our array a indexed by initial position, a_i = c_{P_i} initially. Since the set of values in first k is {P_1..P_k}, the values affected are exactly those with index i<=k. So using a_i is correct.

Let's test with a random small brute force to be absolutely sure. We can't run code, but we can manually simulate a few.

op5: full [1,4,2,5,3]: compare 1<4,4>2 swap [1,2,4,5,3], 4<5,5>3 swap [1,2,4,3,5]. Swaps2, inv1. Total swaps5, ans1. Matches.

Great.

Now, we need output M lines, answer after each operation. Our difference array gives S(t) cumulative swaps. answer = total_inv - S(t). Since total_inv can be up to N(N-1)/2 ~ 2e10, use 64-bit int (Python int fine).

Let's outline implementation:

1. Read N.
2. Read P list length N.
3. Compute a array length N (1-indexed maybe).
   BIT class with add, sum.
   total_inv = 0
   for i, v in enumerate(P, start=1):
       le = bit.sum(v)  # previous <= v (since no v inserted yet, previous < v)
       a_i = (i - 1) - le
       a[i] = a_i
       total_inv += a_i
       bit.add(v, 1)
   Note: bit.sum(v) counts previous values <= v. Since permutation, previous values < v. Good.
4. Read M.
5. Read A list length M.
6. Compute l_i for i=1..N:
   Since A nondecreasing. We can do:
   l = [0]*(N+1)
   t = 0
   for i in range(1, N+1):
       while t < M and A[t] < i:
           t += 1
       if t < M:
           l[i] = t + 1  # 1-based operation index
       else:
           l[i] = M + 1  # never active
   Because A_t >= i after while. For i=1, A[0]>=2>=1, so t=0, l=1.
   Alternatively, since A_i >=2, for i=1 l=1.
7. Build diff array size M+2 (1-indexed) zeros.
   For i in 1..N:
       if a[i] == 0: continue
       start = l[i]
       if start > M: continue
       end = start + a[i] - 1
       if end > M: end = M
       if start <= end:
           diff[start] += 1
           if end + 1 <= M:
               diff[end + 1] -= 1
8. Compute answers:
   cur_swaps = 0
   delta = 0
   ans = []
   for t in range(1, M+1):
       delta += diff[t]
       cur_swaps += delta
       ans.append(str(total_inv - cur_swaps))
   Print '\n'.join(ans).

For t=1, not yet. So no subtract needed. However, if there are multiple intervals ending at M, delta at M includes all; no need to reset after M. So fine.)

So it contributes 1 swap in op1 and 1 swap in op2. Thus Δ(s) should include i4 for s=1 and s=2. Interval [l, l+a-1] = [1,3], truncated to M=2 => [1,2]. So diff[1]+=1, diff[3]-=1 (but 3>M, no need).
