Since end+1=3 > M, no subtract within 1..M, so delta at t=1 and t=2 both include +1. Yes diff1+=1 gives delta1=1, delta2=1 (no subtraction). Good.
i2 interval [1,1]: diff1+=1, diff2-=1.
i6 interval [2,2]: diff2+=1, no subtract.
So diff1 = i4 1 + i2 1 =2. diff2 = i2 -1 + i6 +1 =0. delta1=2, delta2=2. cur S1=2, S2=4. Wait delta2 = delta1 + diff2 =2+0=2. Yes I earlier thought delta2=0 because diff2=0 but delta is cumulative: delta += diff[t]. So delta2 remains 2. cur_swaps = cur + delta =2+2=4. Good. My mistake. So algorithm works.

Need ensure diff array size M+2; if end+1 <= M subtract, else not. Since we only compute up to M, no need to subtract beyond M. But if we don't subtract at M+1, it doesn't affect answers. For delta cumulative, if no subtract, delta stays constant after M, irrelevant. Good.

Let's test sample2? Can't manually, but likely.

Now, let's consider edge cases:
- a_i can be 0 for many; skip.
- l_i = M+1 for positions beyond max A. Since A_i <= N, if max A < N, positions > maxA never active. They contribute 0. In l computation, t reaches M, l=M+1, skip because start>M.
- A_i >=2, so position 1 is always active from first operation. a_1 is always 0? For first element, no larger before, so a_1=0. So no contribution. Good.
- M up to 2e5, N up to 2e5, total_inv up to ~2e10 fits Python int.

Let's verify the formula for l_i with duplicates and nondecreasing A. For position i, first operation with k>=i. Since A sorted, we can compute:
t = 0
for i in 1..N:
    while t < M and A[t] < i:
        t += 1
    l[i] = t+1 if t<M else M+1
Because A is 0-indexed. Example A=[4,6], i=5: t=0, A0=4<5 -> t=1, A1=6>=5, l=2. Good.
i=1: A0=4>=1, l=1.
If A=[2,2,3], i=3: t=0 A0=2<3 t=1 A1=2<3 t=2 A2=3>=3 l=3. Good.

Now, let's think if a_i should be computed as number of larger elements before in initial permutation, but c_v can change not only by decrements but also by larger elements moving from right to left? We argued larger elements never move left across smaller elements. Is that always true? In bubble sort, larger elements move right, smaller move left. But a larger element can move left if it is swapped with an even larger element? Example [5,1,4]: op3 -> [1,4,5]. Value4 moved left? Initial pos3, final pos2? Wait [5,1,4] op3: compare 5>1 swap [1,5,4]; compare 5>4 swap [1,4,5]. Value4 moved left from pos3 to pos2? It swapped with 5 (larger) and moved left. So a larger element (4) moved left across an even larger element (5). Thus larger elements can move left when a larger element passes them. So c for value4? Initial c_4 = number larger before = {5} ->1. After op3, value4 at pos2, larger before? none? Actually array [1,4,5], value4 has no larger before, c=0. So c decreased by1. It moved left by1. So our statement "larger elements move right" is not universally true; an element with c>0 (not a record) moves left, regardless of its value, when a larger record passes it. Thus an element can move left even if it is large relative to others, as long as there is a larger element before it. So c_v decreases by1 for all non-records. Good.

Can c_v ever increase? Suppose a smaller element moves left across v? That would put smaller before v, reducing larger before? Actually if a smaller element moves from right of v to left of v, then v has one fewer smaller before? Wait c_v counts larger before v. If a smaller element moves left across v, it was after v, now before v; it is not larger, so c_v unchanged. If a larger element moves left across v, c_v would increase? But larger elements move left only when an even larger element passes them; can a larger element pass over a smaller element to its right? No, compare-exchange only swaps if left > right, so a larger element can move left only by swapping with an even larger element on its left. That even larger element was already before v? Let's analyze: For value x, a larger value y>x can move left across x only if y is to the left of x and swaps with x? Wait y moves left means y swaps with a larger z to its left, so y moves left relative to z, not relative to x. The set of elements before x can change in order but not in composition? Actually when z passes y, y moves left, but z was already before y and before x? If y is before x, z before y, so z before x. After swap, y before z, both before x. Composition before x unchanged. Thus the set of elements before any given element remains the same? Is that true for all elements? Operation k permutes prefix; for an element inside the prefix, the set of elements before it can change when it swaps with a neighbor. If it swaps with a larger element on its left, the larger element moves from before it to after it, so the set before it loses that larger element (c decreases). If it swaps with a smaller element on its right, the smaller moves from after to before, set before gains a smaller element (c unchanged). It never gains a larger element from the right because swap only if left>right; a larger element on the right would not swap leftward with a smaller left element. It could gain a larger element from the left? That would mean a larger element moves from before to after? Actually if a larger element on the left swaps with it, the larger moves right, so it leaves the before set. Thus the number of larger elements before an element never increases; it decreases by 1 each time a larger element passes it. This is consistent.

Thus c_v is nonincreasing and decremented exactly when v moves left across a larger element. In a bubble pass, every non-record moves left across the record immediately before its block, which is larger. So c--.

Now, let's consider if an element with c>0 could be a record in the prefix? No, record means no larger before in the prefix; since all before are in prefix, c=0. So all c>0 are in blocks and move left. Good.

Now, one more subtlety: The set of values in first k is initial first k, but the c values for those values are not necessarily the initial a_i; they have been decremented by previous operations. Our interval formula accounts for that: each value's current c = a_i - number of previous active operations. It contributes to swaps in operation t iff current c>0, which is iff t <= l_i + a_i -1. Because after d = t - l_i operations before current? Let's check timing:
At start of operation t, value i has been active in operations l_i, l_i+1, ..., t-1. Count = t - l_i. Its c = a_i - (t - l_i) if positive. It contributes if c>0 => a_i - (t - l_i) > 0 => t < l_i + a_i => t <= l_i + a_i -1. This matches interval for Δ(t). After operation t, c becomes a_i - (t - l_i +1). It stops contributing when t = l_i + a_i (after a_i decrements). Good.

Now, let's think about the total swaps S(t) = sum_i min(a_i, t-l_i+1). We compute via Δ intervals. Let's derive Δ(s) = S(s)-S(s-1) = count of i with l_i <= s <= l_i+a_i-1. Yes.

Implementation details:
- Use 0-indexed arrays for positions? We can use 1-indexed for clarity.
- BIT implementation:
  class Fenwick:
    def __init__(self,n): self.n=n; self.bit=[0]*(n+1)
    def add(self, idx, val): while idx<=n: bit[idx]+=val; idx+=idx&-idx
    def sum(self, idx): s=0; while idx>0: s+=bit[idx]; idx-=idx&-idx; return s
- Compute a:
  a = [0]*(N+1)
  total_inv=0
  bit=Fenwick(N)
  for i in range(1,N+1):
      v=P[i-1]
      le = bit.sum(v)  # previous <= v, since no v inserted, previous < v
      ai = (i-1) - le
      a[i]=ai
      total_inv += ai
      bit.add(v,1)
- Compute l:
  l = [0]*(N+1)
  t=0
  for i in range(1,N+1):
      while t < M and A[t] < i:
          t += 1
      l[i] = t+1 if t < M else M+1
- Build diff:
  diff = [0]*(M+2)  # indices 1..M
  for i in range(1,N+1):
      ai = a[i]
      if ai == 0: continue
      start = l[i]
      if start > M: continue
      end = start + ai - 1
      if end > M: end = M
      # start <= end always because start<=M and ai>=1 -> end>=start
      diff[start] += 1
      if end + 1 <= M:
          diff[end+1] -= 1
- Compute answers:
  ans=[]
  delta=0
  cur=0
  for t in range(1,M+1):
      delta += diff[t]
      cur += delta
      ans.append(str(total_inv - cur))
  print('\n'.join(ans))

Now, let's revisit the problem statement: Operation k for i=1..k-1 in this order, if P_i > P_{i+1}, swap. This is exactly one bubble pass on prefix k. We used F(S)=B1 m1 B2 m2... for one pass. Need ensure this transformation is correct for any sequence, not just permutation? It holds for distinct values; P is permutation. Good.

Let's prove F(S) decomposition with LTR maxima:
Let records r_1,...,r_t (values increasing). Let B_s be elements after r_s before r_{s+1}, B_t after r_t. Claim after one pass, sequence becomes B_1 r_1 B_2 r_2 ... B_t r_t.
Proof by induction on t or using the pass algorithm:
During the pass, r_1 is at position 1 initially? Wait r_1 is first element? The first LTR maximum is always the first element of the sequence. Yes r_1 = first element. B_1 after it until r_2. The pass processes:
- Compare r_1 with first element of B_1: since r_1 is larger than all in B_1 (because B_1 elements < r_1? Is that true? B_1 is after r_1 before r_2. Since r_2 is the next record, all elements in B_1 are < r_1? Suppose r_1=3, B_1=[4]? Then 4>3 would be a record, contradiction. So yes B_1 < r_1). Thus r_1 swaps with each element of B_1, moving right through B_1, until it reaches r_2. Then compare r_1 with r_2: r_1 < r_2, no swap. So after processing up to r_2, B_1 has moved before r_1, and r_1 is immediately before r_2. Then r_2 starts moving through B_2, etc. This is exactly the block swap. This proof is intuitive.

Now, the number of swaps in operation k = total length of blocks = k - t = number of elements with c>0 in prefix. Good.

Let's also consider if an element in a block could have c=0 due to larger elements before it outside the prefix? For an element in the prefix, all elements before it are also in the prefix, so no outside. Thus c>0 iff not record. Good.

Now, let's think about the initial a_i computation in relation to values. We compute a_i for position i. But when operation k affects initial first k values, the values are P_1..P_k. Their current c values are not necessarily a_i? Wait a_i is initial c for the value at initial position i. Since the set of values in first k is initial first k, but a value's current c has been decremented by previous operations. Our interval formula uses a_i as the initial c and subtracts active operations. This is correct because each value's c decrements once per operation where its initial position <= current k. Since the value's initial position determines when it becomes active. It doesn't matter that the value may move within the prefix; it remains active for all future operations because future k >= current k and set of first k includes it. So its decrement count is exactly number of operations since activation, capped by a_i. This is independent of its actual position. Excellent.

Let's double-check with a scenario where a value with initial position i becomes active at l_i, but before l_i, could it have been affected by operations with k < i? No, because it is not in first k. Operations with k < i only affect initial positions <=k, which don't include i. Since the set of first k is initial first k, value at initial position i is outside. So no. Thus activation time l_i is correct.

What about operations with k >= i but before l_i? By definition l_i is first such operation. So none.

Thus the entire process decomposes by initial positions independently! This is a beautiful result.

Let's see if we can derive answer formula directly:
For each initial position i, it participates in operations t = l_i, l_i+1, ..., M. It contributes 1 swap in the first a_i of these operations. So total swaps up to t = sum_i max(0, min(a_i, t - l_i + 1)). This is exactly what we compute.

Now, let's ensure total_inv - cur_swaps is the inversion number after operation t. We argued each swap reduces inversion by exactly 1. Initial inversion = total_inv. Since total swaps = cur_swaps, inversion = total_inv - cur_swaps. This is valid because every swap is an adjacent inversion swap and decreases inversion by 1. Good.

Let's consider if operation k can perform a swap that is not between a record and a block element? In bubble pass, every swap is between a record and the first element of a block? Number of swaps equals number of non-records in prefix, not number of block elements? Wait each block element moves left by one, crossing the record. There are k-t block elements. Let's write the final code cleanly.

One thing: The starter code is just ```python```, we need output final Python code in code block. We'll include imports and main.

Let's decide on variable names:

- P
- M
- A

Implementation:

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it)
    P = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]
    M = next(it)
    A = [next(it) for _ in range(M)]

    bit = [0]*(N+1)
    def bit_add(i, v):
        while i <= N:
            bit[i] += v
            i += i & -i
    def bit_sum(i):
        s = 0
        while i > 0:
            s += bit[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return s

    a = [0]*(N+1)
    total_inv = 0
    for idx, val in enumerate(P, 1):
        le = bit_sum(val)  # previous <= val
        ai = (idx - 1) - le
        a[idx] = ai
        total_inv += ai
        bit_add(val, 1)

    first_op = [0]*(N+1)
    t = 0
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        while t < M and A[t] < i:
            t += 1
        if t < M:
            first_op[i] = t + 1
        else:
            first_op[i] = M + 1

    diff = [0]*(M+2)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        ai = a[i]
        if ai == 0:
            continue
        start = first_op[i]
        if start > M:
            continue
        end = start + ai - 1
        if end > M:
            end = M
        diff[start] += 1
        if end + 1 <= M:
            diff[end + 1] -= 1

    out = []
    delta = 0
    swaps = 0
    for t in range(1, M+1):
        delta += diff[t]
        swaps += delta
        out.append(str(total_inv - swaps))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let's think about the proof that the set of values in first k is initial first k even after operations with smaller k: Suppose initial first k set S. Now, let's maybe derive the formula for S(t) one more time to ensure diff intervals are correct:
For each i, contribution to swaps up to t is:
0 if t < l_i
t - l_i + 1 if l_i <= t < l_i + a_i
a_i if t >= l_i + a_i
The increment Δ(t) = contribution(t) - contribution(t-1) is:
1 if l_i <= t <= l_i + a_i - 1
0 otherwise.
So we add +1 to diff at l_i, -1 at l_i + a_i (since interval inclusive ends at l_i+a_i-1, subtract at end+1 = l_i+a_i). In code, end = l_i + a_i - 1; subtract at end+1 = l_i + a_i. We used end+1. Good. If a_i=1, interval [l,l], subtract at l+1. Good.

Now, let's think about if the number of swaps in an operation equals count of active positive c values, but our interval Δ(t) counts values that are active and have c>0 at start of operation. We used condition t <= l_i+a_i-1. At t = l_i+a_i, value has been decremented a_i times in operations l_i..l_i+a_i-1, so c=0 at start of operation t, no contribution. Good.

Now, let's consider if a_i can be 0 but value still contributes to swaps as a record moving right? Swaps are counted for the smaller element that moves left, not for the record. Each swap is counted once. If we counted records moving right, we'd double count. Our count of decremented values counts each swap once. A record with c=0 moving right across a block of size B causes B swaps, and those B swaps are counted as decrements of the B block elements. So record contributes indirectly but not as a decrement. Good.

Now, let's think about if a block element with c>0 could be a "record" in a later operation after its c reaches 0; then it moves right and causes swaps, but those swaps are counted by other elements' decrements. Our model handles each element independently: once its c=0, it no longer decrements, but it may cause decrements to others. Those others have their own a_i and activation times. The interactions are captured by the fact that every element's c decrements once per active operation until zero, regardless of whether it is causing or receiving swaps? Wait, in a swap between a record R and a block element x, x's c decrements. R's c stays. So each swap corresponds to exactly one element's decrement (the block element). Therefore total swaps = total decrements over all elements. Our interval sum counts total decrements over all elements. It doesn't need to model who causes what; the total decrements equals total swaps. And we argued each element's decrement schedule is simply active operations until its initial c exhausted. This is the core. Let's verify with a case where an element with c>0 is in a block and moves left, but the record it crosses might not be the same as the "larger before" counted? It doesn't matter; c decreases by1. The total number of decrements across all elements equals total swaps because each swap decrements exactly one element. Is it possible for a swap to decrement the record's c? No, record c=0. So each swap decrements exactly one element (the smaller one). Thus total swaps = sum of decrements. And each element's decrements are exactly min(a_i, active operations). This is a very elegant invariant.

Let's prove each element's decrements schedule without relying on block decomposition:
- Initially c_i = a_i.
- Each operation where element is in prefix and c_i>0, it is not a record, so it is swapped with the record immediately before its block, moving left by one. This decreases c_i by1.
- If c_i=0, it is a record and does not move left; c_i unchanged.
- If element not in prefix, c_i unchanged.
Thus c_i decreases by1 exactly for each operation t >= l_i until c_i reaches0. Since it starts at a_i, after d active operations c_i = max(0, a_i-d). This is independent of other elements. Perfect.

Now, let's think about if an element with c_i>0 could be in the prefix but not in any block because it is the first element? First element always c=0 (no larger before). So no.

Now, let's consider if an element with c_i>0 could be a record in the prefix if the larger elements before it are outside the prefix? But if element is in prefix, all elements before it are also in prefix (positions < its position <= k). So larger before are in prefix. Thus c_i>0 implies not record. Good.

Now, let's think about if an element outside prefix could have c_i>0 and be a record in some smaller prefix? Not relevant.

Actually operations with length <=k can move an element from position <=k to position <=k (since they only swap within first <=k). They cannot move it beyond k. So current position <=k. Thus it is in the prefix. Good.

Now, let's think about if an element with initial position >k could have moved into first k before operation k due to previous operations with length >k? No previous length >k. So no. Thus prefix set exactly initial first k.

Now, let's think about if the operation k2's bubble pass on the expanded prefix also affects the old prefix elements in a way that depends on the newly included elements. Our model says old prefix elements with c>0 decrement again, regardless. Is that always true? In a bubble pass on the combined prefix, the old prefix elements are at the beginning, and the newly included elements are after them. The pass processes the entire prefix. The old prefix elements may interact with new elements. But the property "every element with c>0 in the prefix moves left by one" holds for the entire prefix, independent of the new elements. Now, let's think about if a value with initial position i and a_i=0 could still have c_i>0 later due to larger elements moving before it from the right? We argued no. Let's prove: Larger elements move right relative to smaller elements; a larger element initially to the right of a smaller element will never cross it to the left because crossing left would require a swap where the larger element is on the right and smaller on left, but compare-exchange only swaps if left > right. Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the proof that each element's c decreases by1 per active operation if c>0. Let's formalize one more time:
In a bubble pass on a sequence, elements with c=0 are LTR maxima. The sequence can be written as m1 B1 m2 B2 ... mt Bt. The pass transforms to B1 m1 B2 m2 ... Bt mt. Therefore every element in any B_s moves one position to the left, crossing m_s. Since m_s is larger than every element in B_s, the number of larger elements before each such element decreases by exactly1. Elements m_s (c=0) move right, no larger element before them appears, so c stays0. Elements outside prefix have the same set of elements before them, so c unchanged. This is rigorous.

data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
ptr=0
N=data[ptr]; ptr+=1
P=data[ptr:ptr+N]; ptr+=N
M=data[ptr]; ptr+=1
A=data[ptr:ptr+M]
This is efficient.

Now, let's think about if the BIT functions should be nested and use `bit` list; yes.

Let's draft final code:

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    ptr = 0
    N = data[ptr]; ptr += 1
    P = data[ptr:ptr+N]; ptr += N
    M = data[ptr]; ptr += 1
    A = data[ptr:ptr+M]

    bit = [0]*(N+1)
    def add(i, v):
        while i <= N:
            bit[i] += v
            i += i & -i
    def sum_(i):
        s = 0
        while i > 0:
            s += bit[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return s

    a = [0]*(N+1)
    total_inv = 0
    for pos, val in enumerate(P, 1):
        less_eq = sum_(val)
        ai = (pos - 1) - less_eq
        a[pos] = ai
        total_inv += ai
        add(val, 1)

    first_op = [0]*(N+1)
    t = 0
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        while t < M and A[t] < i:
            t += 1
        first_op[i] = t + 1 if t < M else M + 1

    diff = [0]*(M+2)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        ai = a[i]
        if ai == 0:
            continue
        start = first_op[i]
        if start > M:
            continue
        end = start + ai - 1
        if end > M:
            end = M
        diff[start] += 1
        if end + 1 <= M:
            diff[end + 1] -= 1

    out = []
    delta = 0
    swaps = 0
    for t in range(1, M+1):
        delta += diff[t]
        swaps += delta
        out.append(str(total_inv - swaps))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let's verify with sample1 using this code: yes.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    ptr = 0

    N = data[ptr]
    ptr += 1
    P = data[ptr:ptr + N]
    ptr += N

    M = data[ptr]
    ptr += 1
    A = data[ptr:ptr + M]

    # Fenwick Tree
    bit = [0] * (N + 1)

    def bit_add(i, v):
        while i <= N:
            bit[i] += v
            i += i & -i

    def bit_sum(i):
        s = 0
        while i > 0:
            s += bit[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return s

    # a[i] = initial number of larger elements before position i
    a = [0] * (N + 1)
    total_inv = 0

    for pos, val in enumerate(P, 1):
        less = bit_sum(val)
        ai = (pos - 1) - less
        a[pos] = ai
        total_inv += ai
        bit_add(val, 1)

    # first_op[i] = first operation index whose k is >= i
    first_op = [0] * (N + 1)
    t = 0
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        while t < M and A[t] < i:
            t += 1
        first_op[i] = t + 1 if t < M else M + 1

    # diff over operation indices: contribution of each position
    diff = [0] * (M + 2)

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        ai = a[i]
        if ai == 0:
            continue

        start = first_op[i]
        if start > M:
            continue

        end = start + ai - 1
        if end > M:
            end = M

        diff[start] += 1
        if end + 1 <= M:
            diff[end + 1] -= 1

    out = []
    delta = 0
    swaps = 0

    for t in range(1, M + 1):
        delta += diff[t]
        swaps += delta
        out.append(str(total_inv - swaps))

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
